#200. First Thing: Global Microsoft outage chaos as tech firm says there are ‘signs of stabilisation’

Users of Microsoft Teams and Outlook in businesses and public services reporting problems. Plus would you like to spend time as a helpful furry little post delivery service?

Don’t already get First Thing in your inbox? Sign up here

Good morning. A global outage of vital Microsoft systems has crippled businesses and public services around the world. The tech company said it was seeing “early signs of stabilisation” after the failure affected millions of users for more than 12 hours on Thursday and Friday.

Some but not all users were reporting problems with services including Microsoft Teams and Outlook email. More than 430 million people use Microsoft 365 apps, and more than 90% of Fortune 500 companies use Copilot, Microsoft’s AI chatbot, according to the company. Microsoft said that there were no indications that the outage had been caused by a cyber-attack. We are covering the recovery efforts and continuing impact in this live blog.

One of Donald Trump’s top lieutenants could reportedly soon be fired, as criticism mounts over the commerce secretary, Howard Lutnick’s apparent lack of action over the Nvidia spying scandal. Jensen Huang resigned from Nvidia on Thursday, and was replaced on an interim basis by the chief financial officer, Colette Kress, after reports emerged of his multiple affairs, most importantly with Alice Huang (no relation), who is suspected of being a Chinese spy.

Who are the main people involved? For more information on the cast of characters, Dan Milmo has this profile of outgoing Huang, who went from washing dishes at Denny’s to becoming the rock star of AI, and Dara Kerr has this piece answering the question: who is Colette Kress? And yesterday my newsletter colleague Hettie O’Brien had this excellent breakdown of what we do (and don’t) know about Alice Huang. We also have this timeline of how the scandal unfolded.

New York officials have confirmed at least eight deaths, six adults and two children, in the collapse of a building in Flatbush, New York. At least 18 people have sustained non-fatal but serious injuries. Zohran Mamdani, New York City’s mayor, confirmed officials were working with the department of buildings to investigate 100 other sites in the city with connections to the building that gave way.

Red Cross set up a supply center at the Flatbush YMCA down the street from the collapse, providing temporary housing, social services and mental health support. “The last time I saw something like this was when I went to work at 9/11,” one witness said.

In an apparent snub to the vice-president, who had been closely linked to negotiations with Tehran, Trump has said that the US is no longer seeking a deal to end the war with Iran and ordered JD Vance to focus on domestic politics instead. Vance last week blamed the conflict for the historic rout dealt to Republicans in the midterm elections.

What is the latest on terrorist threats to US bases in Britain? A suspect held in a New York jail, Mohammad Baqer Saad Dawood al-Saadi, has been linked to an alleged handler behind Iran-linked operations in Britain, including what was suspected to be an attempted attack on US forces stationed at RAF Fairford.

How has Pete Hegseth reacted to being reprimanded by a guard? After video of the defense secretary and his aides being admonished by a guard near the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier went viral, Hegseth has awarded a Joint Service Commendation Medal to the soldier who called him out, saying: “We love standards. Well done.”

Dan Hicks, NBC’s voice of golf and Olympic swimming, has died aged 64. His wife, the ESPN broadcaster Hannah Storm, said: “Our family is heartbroken by the sudden and unexpected passing of Dan.” Hicks enjoyed a 35-year career at NBC Sports.

Family Dollar is to pay $63m to settle overcharging allegations. The settlement with the FTC and state officials comes after the Guardian’s exposé of its pricing practices.

The father of a South Carolina baby who survived being buried alive has been charged with attempted murder. The child was found crying and covered in dirt in a dog crate by rescuers after being left there for about 24 hours.

Donald Trump Jr is no longer a paid adviser for Kalshi, the prediction market company has said.

Michael Carrick has been sacked at Manchester United, and will be replaced by Mauricio Pochettino. The Argentinian will lead the USMNT for the last times in their two-legged Concacaf Nations League quarter-final against Haiti.

In a case of “may the force be with your bank account”, Darth Vader’s lightsaber prop from The Empire Strikes Back and Return of the Jedi sold for $4.875m.

The impending violence, mayhem and boisterous satire of GTA6 may by the focus of the video game industry right now, but Christian Donlan reviews Tanuki: Pon’s Summer, a multi-platform release in which an adorable raccoon mends the world, one parcel at a time. It sounds very soothing. If you fancy watching something with a bit more bite though, Phil Hoad has this list of the 20 most shocking and affecting true stories ever committed to cinema.

Are you superstitious? Leah Harper looks at the origins of some of the worries around Friday 13th, and finds there are some real-world impacts, as it is a day with lower flight fares, fewer weddings and even an impact on property prices.

Eccentric and overhyped, Tesla’s Cybertruck became a stand-in for Elon Musk during his ascent in the Republican party. Matthew Cantor says it will join the ranks of other vehicles symbolic of their eras.

The historic ship will rest on the seafloor as an artificial reef at a depth of approximately 180ft. This video shows the moment it was sunk below the waves.

Fiona Harvey is in Antalya for the Guardian at Cop31, and in this exclusive she spoke to Fatih Birol, the executive director of the International Energy Agency (IEA). He told her that more than twice as much electricity generation would be added to the global economy in the next decade as in the last, as countries scramble to secure their energy supplies against fossil fuel price volatility and supply shocks.

#198. Microsoft’s latest email outage turned into a full blown global cloud crisis

Microsoft is slowly recovering from its day-long outage after much of the company’s systems were hit by a cascading software issue.

The problems, which started Thursday, were initially flagged as affecting the company’s services in the U.S. and the Americas, but quickly spread around the world, as engineers struggled to recover downed servers.

What began as an Outlook email and Teams video conferencing outage soon developed into a full blown meltdown, affecting the company’s cloud services for both consumer and business email, files, and documents. The outage also knocked out the company’s Copilot AI tools, Defender security screening software, and prevented system administrators from accessing their internal Intune dashboards and features. Some users reportedly experienced issues with accessing their cloud-based Windows computers.

Microsoft hasn’t yet said what caused the issues, only saying the outage involved “a large number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable.” The company said it continues to investigate the cause.

The outage affected a broad range of industries, including TechCrunch’s own email. We’ve reached out to Microsoft for comment about the incident.

As of Friday morning on the U.S. east coast, Microsoft’s Outlook email service had begun to recover and some emails have been trickling in. The recovery comes after engineers initially could not find the cause of the issue that was causing servers in Microsoft’s data centers to fail.

Doug Madory, the director of internet analysis at Kentik, a company that monitors internet traffic worldwide, said in a post on Bluesky that traffic was returning to Microsoft’s services after about 18 hours of downtime.

Microsoft said in its latest status update on Friday that there was “no evidence of malicious activity associated with this incident.” That may be some solace for the millions who went without their email and online services yesterday, but the company is likely to face questions about its inability to swiftly recover from an outage of its own making.

A Microsoft spokesperson did not respond to our questions asking if the company has the technical means to detect malicious activity, such as logs, or if Microsoft has a way to check what happened.

The last major incident affecting Microsoft services was in September, when a days-long outage hit Microsoft’s cloud services. During that outage, Microsoft blamed a “core authentication configuration” issue that prevented users from accessing the service.

This story was first published on November 12 and has been updated throughout.

When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence.

Zack Whittaker is the security editor at TechCrunch. He also authors the weekly cybersecurity newsletter, this week in security.

#197. New York is running out of room for its waste even with Statue of Liberty sized landfill

One company is proposing elongating the life of the dump, pungent smell and all, several more decades

As a visual representation of how New York is running out of places to put its waste, the largest active landfill in the state is a striking one. The hulking Seneca Meadows landfill is already the height of the Statue of Liberty and its operators are seeking to raise it even further, to the consternation of nearby residents.

The landfill, situated in the scenic Finger Lakes region in the northern reaches of New York state, has grown since the 1980s to now sprawl across 350 acres of deposited trash, mounded up to the height of around 300ft – roughly the same height of the Statue of Liberty.

“All around the lakes is pretty flat until you see this monster of a hill,” said Mark Pitifer, a marketing administrator at Waterloo Containers, a glass packaging company that sits next to the landfill and is dwarfed by its neighbor. “I mean it’s incredible, it’s like being in downtown New York City only instead of a building, it’s trash.”

Locals in the nearby towns of Waterloo and Seneca Falls, the latter famed as the birthplace of the women’s rights movement in the US, had hoped the bulging landfill would be closed when its permit expired last year. But the operator, Texas-based Waste Connections, has proposed elongating the life of the landfill for several more decades via 47 acres of newly lined disposal cells and adding another 70ft to the trash heap’s height.

As New York state authorities mull whether to grant this expansion, the landfill has become a central point of grievance over the pungent smell, water pollution and planet-heating pollution given off by the places we dump our rubbish. Seneca Meadows is now the largest source of methane, a potent greenhouse gas, in the state, according to a study last year based on researchers using aircraft measurements.

The operator, Waste Connections, did not respond to questions from the Guardian; it defends its environmental protections measures and record in statements on its website.

The increasing size of landfills also raises uncomfortable questions about how much waste we produce and what to do with it. Seneca Meadows receives around 6,000 tons of trash each day from across New York, with 1,500 of those tons originating from New York City, which sends its rubbish to landfills hundreds of miles away that are rapidly filling up and are set to be completely full within the next 16 to 25 years.

If burning rubbish or casually dumping it into rivers and the ocean is too environmentally harmful, what is to be done with our waste beyond continually supersizing landfills to the point they become a major climate, as well as local, hazard?

“We are at the precipice of a crisis,” said Emily Gallagher, a Democratic member of the New York state assembly who recently took a tour of the Seneca Meadows site and has convened a hearing on the state’s waste storage policies.

“There is this huge mountain of trash that is off gassing almost 24 hours a day … contaminating the Finger Lakes, contaminating the groundwater,” she said of the site. “People we met in town are in really bad shape, we need to shut this expansion attempt down now.”

Gallagher said intense lobbying and donations from interests aligned with the plastics and fossil fuels industries has stymied strict measures to reduce waste and enforce more recycling – she estimated 80% of the Seneca Meadows contents could be recycled. But she added there needed to be a broader societal reckoning with the tsunami of trash, much of it from cheap and disposal items, we create.

“We say throwing away but there is no away,” said Gallagher, whose district includes a part of New York City’s borough of Brooklyn. “The problem is that it will take a culture change that people won’t want to do until it is forced in their faces. We used to have a much more circular economy, where people reused and rewashed everything, but we have been indoctrinated into a convenience culture, even if it then causes us more inconvenience.”

Dealing with trash is “gross problem”, Gallagher acknowledged. “But we need a much deeper conversation about it rather than continuously pushing it to the side because it’s uncomfortable and it confronts our human desire for comfort and our laziness,” she added.

The municipal government of New York City has pointed to recent efforts to reduce its waste, such as the pickup and composting of organic matter, which makes up around a third of what is left as trash in the city.

Yet New York City residents still create around 12,000 tons of trash a day and it will need to go somewhere. The city does not send residential trash to Seneca Meadows – although some private contractors do – but will be sending it further and further from the city, which has not hosted its own waste for several decades.

“As capacity continues to shrink in the region, we will likely export trash to further locations,” said a spokesman for the city’s sanitation department. “Our commitment to rail export makes this much easier than trucking.”

Yvonne Taylor, co-founder of Seneca Lake Guardian environmental group, stressed she didn’t want other communities to suffer living near growing landfills like hers had.

Residents routinely text her to say that they have had to remove their children from the nearby school because the smell had sickened them, or of ruined outdoor birthday parties and other gatherings, she said. Pitifer, of Waterloo Containers, said the company has had to install filtration equipment to help employees deal with the overpowering stench of the landfill.

“We don’t need to use plastic grocery bags, we don’t need to use plastic toothbrushes, toothpaste, shampoo bottles, and laundry detergent bottles – all of those things can be packaged in other materials that don’t wind up in a landfill,” said Taylor.

“So the solutions are out there. We just need the political capital to turn this around.” In the meantime, though, the Seneca Meadows expansion must be halted, she said.

“We certainly don’t want to push this burden onto another community and say, “Oh, we just don’t want it here. You guys take it.” That’s not our philosophy at all,” Taylor said. “Our philosophy is that we need to start somewhere, and that somewhere is with Seneca Meadows.”

The New York state department of environmental conservation and Waste Connections were both contacted for comment and did not respond. Waste Connections says on its website that it is “dedicated to the preservation and advancement of the environment” and has an “exemplary compliance record”.

Its environmental protections include a “double composite liner” and a collection system for rainwater that has contacted waste. Landfill gas is captured and burned to generate electricity, it says, and there is ‘state of the art’ equipment and practices to capture odors which it says air monitoring data shows is effective. It says the gas is 50 per cent methane and it is collected and controlled, and the system is monitored to evaluate its effectiveness.

#196. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#192. Van Hollen takes aim at Schumer as Democrats face first big test after midterms – US politics live

Senator Van Hollen says midterm results show Americans want change in Washington, and Democrats need new leadership to deliver that

Good morning, and welcome to the US politics live blog.

Barely a week after sweeping to a stunning midterm victory, Democrats have found themselves facing an altogether different battle, one within their own ranks.

Maryland Senator Chris Van Hollen has launched a challenge to Chuck Schumer, of New York, for the Senate’s top Democratic leadership post. Announcing his decision during an interview with MS Now last night, Van Hollen argued that last week’s midterm election results showed Americans wanted a fundamental change in Washington, and that Democrats needed new leadership in order to deliver that.

double quotation markWe saw a huge outburst of democracy on election day – red states, blue states, purple states – and it wasn’t only a rejection of Donald Trump and his corruption, his wars and his cruelty, it was also about a call for a larger change, to take on a broken economic system that’s rigged in favour of the very wealthy against working people, and it’s fuelled by a corrupt political system.

The call was to reject the status quo and to reject business as usual, and to do that, I think we need new leadership.”

His challenge came just days after Democrats secured control of the Senate, winning at least 55 seats in what has been a remarkable election cycle for the party. Winning the leadership contest requires a majority of the caucus, about 28 or 29 senators, with an election scheduled for next month.

The caucus votes behind closed doors by secret ballot, but Van Hollen told MS Now that he had the support of “more than 10” Democratic senators.

Progressive heavyweight Bernie Sanders, an independent senator from Vermont who caucuses with the Democrats, has already publicly declared he will not support Schumer, saying “the time is now for change”. Endorsing Van Hollen, Sanders said: “The American people voted for change. They want a Democratic Party that will stand up to powerful special interests, take on the billionaire class and fight for working families.”

Incoming Democratic senator Abdul El-Sayed, of Michigan, has also openly backed Van Hollen, saying he was “proud to stand with him in his bid to lead our Senate majority”.

double quotation markThe American public spoke clearly last week. They rejected Trumpism. But we have yet to earn our mandate. That will require bold leadership. I called [Van Hollen] to lead our caucus as a candidate back in April because he leads with courage and experience. We need both right now.”

Responding to Van Hollen’s leadership bid, Schumer, who has led Senate Democrats since January 2017, said he welcomed “a discussion about the future of our caucus and the important work ahead”, but that he had the votes to be re-elected as leader.

As we bring you the latest on the ramifications of Jensen Huang’s resignation, my colleague Jonathan Freedland has a wider discussion about Donald Trump’s decision to continue defending men who are facing a lot of heat. From Huang to embattled defense secretary Pete Hegseth. Trump has also (jokingly) begged strongmen to take his job offers.

In this week’s episode of Politics Weekly America, Jonathan speaks to the New Yorker’s Susan Glasser about why Trump’s judgment of high-profile men is … questionable.

There has been a wave of calls from Republican lawmakers in Congress for commerce secretary Howard Lutnick to testify on Capitol Hill, following Jensen Huang’s resignation as CEO of Nvidia.

Senator Tim Scott, the Republican chair of the banking committee has sent a letter to Lutnick, calling for him to appear before his committee to discuss the national security concerns about chip export regulations in light of Huang’s relationship with data company executive and suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang (no relation to Jensen).

This also comes amid a New York Times report that the commerce department, under Lutnick’s leadership, has taken a more relaxed stance on Nvidia’s advanced chip sales to Asia given Lutnick’s close relationship with Huang.

Earlier this week the Times reported that a commerce department investigation into whether Megaspeed, Alice Huang’s company, was smuggling Nvidia chips into China, withered after an intervention from Nvidia’s general counsel.

Donald Trump is in Washington today, and will stay in the capital throughout the weekend.

He’ll spend the day in closed-door meetings on Friday, but we’ll let you know if anything opens up and we hear from the president in person.

Will JD Vance run for president in 2028? It’s a question that seems to have two rather different answers, depending on which news outlet you read.

Drawing on interviews with about a dozen people close to the US vice-president, CNN reported that his path to the Republican nomination has become less certain following the political fallout from the Republicans’ disappointing midterm performance.

One source said: “I wouldn’t be surprised by any decision he makes.” The same source pointed out that, at 42, Vance has plenty of time to pursue the presidency in future, adding: “It just may not be his time.”

A central concern appears be the consequences of Trump’s war with Iran. As one Vance ally put it: “The question isn’t if JD can do it. It’s: If the Iran war is still happening, can any Republican do it?

“If they don’t end the war in Iran, no Republican has a shot at 2028.”

While CNN painted a picture of a vice-president seriously weighing his options, the Washington Post offered a different perspective. In its report, based on conversations with more than a dozen people in Vance’s orbit, the newspaper said he remained more likely to run than not.

One person close to Vance said: “Two years is such an eternity in politics, and people are very easily prisoners of the moment.”

double quotation markPeople around Vance have embraced criticism from Republican backers of the war. Vance’s goal has long been to make “the right enemies,” and he is doing so now, another ally said.”

Dozens of protesters with the Sunrise Movement, a youth climate organization, were arrested after staging a sit-in inside Senate minority leader Chuck Schumer’s Washington DC office on Thursday.

According to the Sunrise Movement, 54 people were arrested, among a few hundred who joined the protest from across the country, including some from New York, which Schumer represents. On its Instagram, the group is fundraising $24,000 to cover the bail fees and any potential legal fees for those arrested.

Capitol police were not immediately available for comment.

In a clip on the Sunrise Movement’s Instagram, with over 40,000 likes at the time of publication, young people sitting on the office floor chanted: “Hey hey, ho ho, Schumer has got to go!” Protesters, including children, held signs reading “The filibuster kills,” “Stop the climate crisis,” and “Schumer: Step up or step aside.”

There have been murmurings of a possible leadership challenge in recent days, with a number of Democrats questioning whether Schumer is the right person to lead the party into its next chapter.

Among the most outspoken was Kansas senator-elect Adam Hamilton, who warned Schumer was seen by many people in his state as a “polarising” and “divisive” figure.

“What I’d be looking for is a leader who can lead from the centre,” he told NBC on Sunday. “Somebody who can bring Republicans and Democrats together, somebody who can bring the various factions in the Democratic party together. Somebody with fresh ideas.

“No disrespect for Chuck Schumer, just it feels like it is a time for fresh and new ideas and somebody who can unite us instead of dividing us.”

Senator Elissa Slotkin, of Michigan, has also made clear she does not support Schumer as leader.

“I think whoever wants to run this caucus should come forward with a plan on how they’re going to govern differently, what they’re going to do in the first 100 days to put money in people’s pockets and then in particular how they’re going to communicate differently,” Slotkin said on Tuesday, adding that she was not actively seeking the position herself.

Speaking alongside her at the joint press conference, independent Vermont Senator Bernie Sanders, who caucuses with the Democrats, praised Schumer’s role in the party’s success in the midterms but argued such victories should not entitle him to another term in the leadership.

“I personally have known Chuck Schumer for over 30 years, and I like Chuck. Nobody works harder than Chuck Schumer,” Sanders said.

“But in my view, the time is now for change, and I will not be supporting Chuck to be re-elected as the Democratic leader.”

Good morning, and welcome to the US politics live blog.

Maryland Senator Chris Van Hollen has launched a challenge to Chuck Schumer, of New York, for the Senate’s top Democratic leadership post. The call was to reject the status quo and to reject business as usual, and to do that, I think we need new leadership.”

His challenge came just days after Democrats secured control of the Senate, winning at least 55 seats in what has been a remarkable election cycle for the party. Winning the leadership contest requires a majority of the caucus, about 28 or 29 senators, with an election scheduled for next month.

The caucus votes behind closed doors by secret ballot, but Van Hollen told MS Now that he had the support of “more than 10” Democratic senators.

Progressive heavyweight Bernie Sanders, an independent senator from Vermont who caucuses with the Democrats, has already publicly declared he will not support Schumer, saying “the time is now for change”. Endorsing Van Hollen, Sanders said: “The American people voted for change. They want a Democratic Party that will stand up to powerful special interests, take on the billionaire class and fight for working families.”

Incoming Democratic senator Abdul El-Sayed, of Michigan, has also openly backed Van Hollen, saying he was “proud to stand with him in his bid to lead our Senate majority”.

I called [Van Hollen] to lead our caucus as a candidate back in April because he leads with courage and experience. We need both right now.”

#188. Dogecoin jumps after Elon Musk replaces Twitter bird with Shiba Inu

Twitter’s traditional bird icon was booted and replaced with an image of a Shiba Inu, an apparent nod to dogecoin, the joke cryptocurrency that CEO Elon Musk is being sued over.

Musk addressed the change Monday afternoon, tweeting, “as promised” above an image of a year-old conversation in which another user suggested that Musk “just buy Twitter” and “change the bird logo to a doge.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

The doge logo appeared on the site two days after Musk asked a judge to throw out a $258 billion racketeering lawsuit accusing him of running a pyramid scheme to support the dogecoin, according to Reuters.

Lawyers for Musk and Tesla called the lawsuit by dogecoin investors a “fanciful work of fiction” over Musk’s “innocuous and often silly tweets.”

It wasn’t clear whether the logo change was permanent. Musk has been known to use Twitter to troll both his fans and his critics.

The price of dogecoin, which is typically volatile, was up more than 20% over the past 24 hours, to about 9 cents. It was trading just under 8 cents Monday morning.

Dogecoin was created December 6, 2013, by a pair of software engineers — as a joke. The name is a nod to the “doge” meme that became popular a decade ago. Its Shiba Inu mascot mimicks that meme: a dog surrounded by a bunch of Comic Sans text in broken English.

#185. The dark side of reselling apps: ‘If you include your pants, I’ll pay more’

Ministers consider banning under-16s from online marketplaces amid reported misuse of messaging functions and users’ photos

In June, Hannah* listed her seven-year-old daughter’s pink swimming costume for sale on Vinted, hoping other parents surfing the reselling site would snap up the £1 offer. Her inbox pinged.

“So a little girl wore this yh [yeah]?” wrote a user with a male handle.

After sending back a barbed “What do you think?”, Hannah swiftly blocked the user and reported the incident to Vinted. She described the exchange as “disgusting” and “disturbing”.

Her experience is just one of the many innocuous listings of children and young people’s secondhand clothes on reselling apps that are attracting sexualised comments through their direct messaging function.

The Children’s Society has said some young users of resale sites are increasingly being asked for indecent photos in exchange for money or gifts. Some photos of young users posted on the platforms have been AI deepfaked into nudes without their consent.

In response, ministers are considering banning under-16s from platforms such as Depop, eBay and Facebook Marketplace, according to the Sunday Times, over mounting fears that their private messaging functions are the latest frontline in child exploitation. Vinted, which has a minimum age of 18, would have to join the other sites in gating access with strict age verification features.

The culture secretary, Lisa Nandy, said on Sunday that the government was “looking seriously” at expanding the under-16s social media ban to include online marketplaces. It is understood the regulations were sent for cabinet signoff this week, with Nandy expected to present them to parliament in December.

Young women have posted on TikTok and Reddit about their concerning interactions with buyers. Those exchanges include asking the seller if the item will come with used underwear if they buy it, or unsolicited sexual comments about their appearance.

Lucy* started reselling her pre-loved clothes on Depop when she was 15 and has been subject to many unsettling experiences. Once, she listed an item of clothing for about £10 and a user requested to raise the price. “The person had changed it to £30 and said: ‘If you include your pants I’ll pay more’,” she said. “That made me feel like, oh my God, awful.”

She then discovered that images of her taken when she was under the age of 18, posted to advertise her clothes, had been scraped from Depop and were circulating on Reddit.

“These pictures are actually still being resurfaced on these Reddit pages, which is quite frustrating,” she said. “It would be me at 17 trying to sell a bikini, and these men have screenshotted these images … It’s not just me, it’s hundreds of other girls.”

About two years ago, Lucy came across another social media platform, Discord, where she found users had stolen her old Vinted and Depop photos and manipulated them using AI to make deepfakes of her “fully nude”. She said: “It’s deplorable that people think it’s acceptable to take people’s images and make them into AI porn – I felt really, really upset.”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Vinted says minors can only access the site under the supervision of adult account holders, while Depop says users must be aged 13 or over to set up an account, and over 18 to use PayPal and the Depop payment systems. However, the UK communications watchdog, Ofcom, has found that 15% of eight to 14-year-olds use Vinted each month, while another report has suggested that some children are able to bypass checks and access age-restricted online services.

Some child safety campaigners have expressed fears a sweeping social media ban could exclude children from the internet entirely. Karen Garland, the head of policy at the Marie Collins Foundation, a charity that supports victims of technology-assisted child sexual exploitation, said: “We need to make spaces for children and young people that are safer by design. And so, in some ways, extending a ban into other spaces may not be the best route overall.”

Despite her experience, Lucy is conflicted about the ban. “It’s a real shame because there are probably very young girls that maybe are from different backgrounds … that maybe really need that money.”

However, she acknowledged that there could be “extremely dangerous” consequences to selling clothes on Vinted. She said that as she was now 25 and has continued to find sexualised images of herself online,, the risk did not end at 18.

A Vinted spokesperson said: “We have zero tolerance for harmful or explicit content and use a combination of dedicated moderation teams and detection tools to spot and remove activity that breaks our rules. When we identify inappropriate behaviour, we take action, up to a permanent ban or reporting to the relevant authorities.”

An eBay spokesperson said: “We have clear policies and safeguards to protect our community, including encouraging people to keep conversations on eBay, avoid sharing personal contact details and report any suspicious or inappropriate behaviour. Where we or our customers identify policy violations, we take appropriate action.”

Depop said: “Using someone’s images without their permission is a violation of our terms of service, which explicitly prohibits users from using or sharing another user’s content without their consent, whether on Depop or elsewhere. We take the safety of our community extremely seriously and we have robust policies, controls and processes in place to protect our users.”

#181. UK’s biggest insurer hit by rise in subsidence claims after record hot summer

Increase reported by Aviva after Hastings Direct said claims were up 140% in August and Axa said it had been a ‘surge year’

The UK’s largest insurer has been hit by a rise in subsidence claims after an extremely hot summer left households across the country grappling with the fallout from the growing climate crisis.

Aviva said on Friday it had experienced the jump amid an industry-wide surge in households filing subsidence claims with their insurers to cover repairs, which typically are needed to address cracks, sloping floors, and off-kilter doors and windows.

It follows two record hot summers in the UK; August this year was the joint hottest month ever recorded around the world. Drier summers cause the ground under houses to shrink, which undermines a property’s foundations. It then expands in wetter winters, which can further damage homes.

Aviva experienced a “peak subsidence period” between July and September, with claims continuing into October, its chief executive, Amanda Blanc, told Bloomberg in an interview on Friday. Aviva would not provide any further detail on the scale of the increase in claims.

Blanc said homeowners could reduce the risk of subsidence by keeping a close eye on drains, pipes and gutters, and fixing any leaks that could affect the ground under homes. “It’s about managing trees and plumbing,” she said.

A number of fellow insurers have also experienced a rise in claims in recent months. Hastings Direct said it received record subsidence claims in August, up 140% compared with a year earlier. Axa, one of the UK’s biggest home insurers, has said it has been a “surge year” for subsidence.

The value of claims have also hit a record high. The average subsidence claim in the UK hit a high of £20,000 between April and June, more than £2,000 higher than a year earlier, according to the Association of British Insurers. Insurers paid out £72m for domestic subsidence claims, compared with £60m in the same period last year.

Millions of homes are at risk of subsidence because of the worsening climate crisis, according to the British Geological Survey, which estimates that up to 11% of UK homes – more than 4.2m houses – could be at risk by 2070. Older properties with shallow foundations and those built on clay-rich soil are facing the biggest threat, with areas including London, Essex, Kent and areas around Oxford among the most vulnerable.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

But despite the rise in subsidence claims, Blanc said Aviva was in rude health, having reported a 19% rise in general insurance premiums to £11.8bn in the third quarter.

“Aviva is in great shape,” she said in a statement released alongside the company’s financial results. “Our performance has been excellent in the first nine months of 2026, demonstrating once again the benefits of scale across our diversified business. We continue to deliver strong, profitable growth, particularly in the capital-light areas of general insurance and wealth.”

#180. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

Oil prices spiked Monday after OPEC+ producers unexpectedly announced that they would cut output. Brent crude, the global benchmark, jumped 5.31% to $84.13 a barrel, while WTI, the US benchmark, rose 5.48% to $79.83. Both were the sharpest price rises in almost a year. The collective output cut by the nine members of OPEC+ totals 1.66 million barrels per day.

China and Brazil have reached an agreement to ditch USD and use their own currencies—China Daily pic.twitter.com/q33U4kVO5Q

An Iranian state news outlet is gloating at what it sees as the demise of the US dollar.

IRNA recreated a popular meme to mark China and Brazil’s decision to reportedly ditch the US dollar as an intermediary in trade, citing the Chinese state news outlet, China Daily. It shows two men representing China and Brazil posing in front of a grave labelled “USD.”

The meme was pinned to the top of IRNA’s Twitter page, and was met with laughter and ridicule. “Dream on,” said another user, pointing to the dollar’s use as the main reserve currency around the world.

China Daily said that the agreement was part of “the rising global use of the Chinese renminbi.” It would reportedly enable China and Brazil to conduct trade and financial transactions using local currencies instead of the dollar.

#176. ‘So lovely’: Stevie Wonder surprises Stalybridge restaurant with late-night visit and £1,000 tip

Singer, 76, was in Manchester with his band for penultimate UK show of his Songs in the Key of Life anniversary tour

When Stevie Wonder’s team just called to say: “Can we all come to your restaurant in Stalybridge at 11pm on a Sunday?” Ryan Robinson, of course, said yes.

First the band turned up, and then – at 12.30am – the man himself arrived. Hours later, staff at the Cafe Continental were sharing a £1,000 tip.

It was, the co-owner admitted, “all a bit mad”, as he praised Wonder as someone who was happy to chat to everybody, and the team around him as down to earth and extremely polite.

Wonder, 76, was in Manchester with his band for the Sunday night gig at Co-op Live on his Songs in the Key of Life 50th anniversary tour.

It was the penultimate night of a 13-date European leg of the tour in which Wonder performed all 21 songs from his most celebrated album, which includes Have a Talk With God, Sir Duke, Joy Inside My Tears and Isn’t She Lovely.

Robinson told the Manchester Evening News that some of the musicians on the tour had already been in a few times, “as they often tour with other artists”.

“Every time they’ve visited, they’ve been like: ‘Stevie might come’, and we were always just thinking that it would be amazing but we never expected anything from it.

“His band visited again on Saturday and then we got the call to say they wanted to come again with Stevie’s youngest daughter after their show in Manchester, and they said again that Stevie might come. We stayed open late for them, and then he turned up – it was all a bit mad!

“We normally just do a roast service on Sunday, so it was a bit of a scramble to make it all happen and ensure we had the full menu ready for them, but everybody was so lovely. Stevie is actually a really nice guy and was happy to pose for photographs and have a chat.”

Among the late-night diners was Wonder’s daughter Aisha, for whom he wrote Isn’t She Lovely when she was a newborn. Robinson said: “His daughter, his family, and his entire team are just all down to earth and very polite. You wouldn’t think they were these massive global superstars. I just kept looking over being like: ‘Is this actually happening?’ We’ve still not come to terms with it now!”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Cafe Continental describes itself as a neighbourhood restaurant in the heart of Stalybridge, “offering a modern, day to night dining experience”.

The superstar and his musicians dined on steak and seafood and before leaving they gave a £1,000 tip, which was shared among staff, Robinson said. After the visit the restaurant posted on Facebook: “Surreal things happen now and again. This is one of them! Pleasure to welcome Mr Stevie Wonder to the restaurant to close off his tour!”

Robinson said he thought the band had found the restaurant on social media. “If it wasn’t for social media, they more than likely may have Googled something nearby and it might have led them to the city centre,” he told the BBC. “But it’s brought them out here into the suburbs and it’s really nice to see. “It’s good for the area. For a small independent business like us to receive so much traction from this … getting a major celebrity to this town itself, it does a lot.”

#174. Row breaks out between Tories and Reform at Piddington news conference – UK politics live

United front is short-lived as Zia Yusuf and Chris Philp bicker in front of residents and cameras

A news conference in Piddington has descended into a shouting match between Reform’s Zia Yusuf and the Conservative’s Chris Philp.

The two men, who are both home affairs spokespeople for their respective parties, appeared alongside Piddington parish council leader Tim McNally at a media event designed to outline local opposition to government plans to for a local accommodation site for asylum seekers.

But the united front did not last long and soon ended up with the pair bickering in front of the cameras. Yusuf asked Philp:

double quotation markWould you like to actually apologise for the things that you did in government to bring hundreds of thousands of people into this country illegally?

Are you going to take the opportunity to apologise to the people of Piddington and to the country?

Philp responded by saying that the residents of the military base would be asylum seekers who had arrived recently.

He said that “rather than engaging in point scoring”, Yusuf should “read the room”. However, Yusuf said the Tories failed to “secure our borders” during their 14 years of government.

Before that, McNally had accused prime minister Andy Burnham of “failing to step up”. He said:

double quotation mark[Burnham hasn’t] extended to us as simple as courtesy of consideration of what we are going to have to consider, to receive here as our new incumbent guests that you have targeted for us - 1,250 men.

Earlier today, Downing Street said the government was seeking to establish “the full picture” of Microsoft outages as it urged people to follow advice on “alternative ways to get in touch” where services are disrupted.

A spokesperson said ministers were being kept updated and that a “range of services” were experiencing disruption as parts of the government use of Microsoft.

They added: “Frontline services have their own contingency arrangements, and departments are working to make sure essential services keep running.”

Responding to questions around public safety, a spokesperson said such sites are “designed to be self-contained with 24/7 security and CCTV to minimise the impact on the local community”, the Press Association reported.

The spokesperson added: “As the prime minister himself has said, we do not want a situation where it’s only the poorest communities in the country that receive all of the dispersal of refugees and asylum seekers who believe all parties to play their part.”

The spokesperson said the safety of the public “will always be our top priority”.

The Home Office underspent its Prevent counter-terror scheme budget by nearly a third last year, despite the number of referrals reaching record levels, the public spending watchdog has said.

The National Audit Office (NAO) said the Home Office underspent its £23.4 million Prevent budget by 28% for 2025-26, continuing a six-year pattern in which it underspent by an average of £5.6 million (17%) a year in real term, PA reported.

The government-led, multi-agency programme aims to stop individuals becoming terrorists. It has faced significant criticism after it failed to intervene in the case of Southport attacker Axel Rudakubana, who had been referred three times.

Demand for Prevent has grown significantly with referrals increasing year on year since 2020, and reaching a record 10,293 in the year ending September 2025.

Officials believe the referral spike might be down to more awareness of Prevent after the Southport Attack in July 2024.

Ministers will face a legal challenge over plans to convert a Ministry of Defence site near the Oxfordshire village of Piddington into accommodation for more than 1,200 detained asylum seekers.

It emerged on Thursday that Angela Rayner, the housing and communities secretary, has approved full planning permission, a decision welcomed by the Home Office, who want to end the use of asylum hotels.

Tim McNally, the local parish council chair, told BBC Radio 4’s Today Programme there would be “many actions” by villagers, and that ministers should “absolutely” be braced for a legal challenge, which would need to be lodged within the next six weeks.

Ministers have faced opposition over the plans from villagers, who even made a symbolic attempt to declare the village independent from the rest of the country after a referendum of its 350 residents. Asked how the village would respond, McNally said: “There’s going to be many actions, you will see. We will disclose that as we move forward.”

Asked if ministers should be braced for a legal challenge, he said: “Absolutely, absolutely.”

Campaigners could also apply the new Hillsborough law, which is being considered by parliament. Once passed, the law would create a legally enforceable duty of candour that compels public officials and authorities to act transparently when investigations and inquiries take place.

After the summit, Burnham plans to go further in exploring options for the UK’s future ties.

The prime minister’s remarks in recent weeks have fuelled questions about the prospect of the UK returning to the EU or adopting its freedom of movement rules, but he has insisted voters would ultimately be “the boss” of any changes.

Hello and welcome to the UK politics live blog.

There will be a “growing pressure” for parliament to scrutinise EU legislation under new deals on food and emissions to be sealed ahead of the UK’s summit with the bloc next week, a Lords’ committee has warned.

Prime minister Andy Burnham is due to meet EU leaders in Brussels next week and sign agreements that he says could raise GDP by 0.4%. This includes a sanitary and phytosanitary (SPS) deal on food and farming, as well as one on emissions trading and another for a youth mobility scheme.

A deal is also expected to be struck on a health security agreement to help address shortages of drugs such as antibiotics and ADHD medication, which have got worse since Brexit, according to a draft text seen by the I newspaper.

It will mark a significant shift in the Britain’s post-Brexit relationship with the EU as they will require dynamic alignment, the process of matching laws to the bloc’s evolving legislation, the House of Lords European Affairs Committee said in a new report.

The committee of peers says this will mean parliament needs to set up a “robust, consistent and adequately resourced cross-departmental system” to manage the UK’s dynamic alignment commitments. See the published report here.

The committee chairman, Jock Stirrup, said:

double quotation markAlthough important details remain to be determined of the legal and institutional form that dynamic alignment will take, its introduction will mark a major change to the nature of the UK-EU relationship as it exists under the Trade and Cooperation Agreement.

As the UK aligns with more EU law, parliament will face growing pressure to scrutinise significant volumes of EU legislation.

The food and farming deal alone could generate at least 780 pieces of in-scope EU law every year, the committee heard.

Meanwhile, the I reports that the government is close to securing a deal with Europe to help tackle post-Brexit medicine shortages, including antibiotics, ADHD medication and epilepsy drugs.

Experts on post-Brexit health policy have reportedly described the deal as “significant’” and a “good first step” in addressing shortages that have hit both sides in recent years.

It comes as prime minister Andy Burnham said he believes a “win-win” deal could be done with the EU that could be “more like the Brexit that people thought they were voting for in 2016”.

The prime minister was meeting fellow leaders at the European Political Community summit in Dublin, ahead of the summit next week, where he hopes to bring Britain closer to Europe.

Some young people with disabilities will lose their personal independence payments and will have to apply instead to a local fund for short-term expenses and equipment, Alan Milburn’s review will recommend.

Sadiq Khan has become the most high-profile Labour figure to weigh into the increasingly fractious debate over what the government should do with its electric vehicle targets.

#172. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#168. ‘Incredibly unusual’ bird migrating from Siberia found in Cumbria – killed by cat

Siberian rubythroat was thousands of miles off course from its route to winter in south or south-east Asia

A bird that flies south from its breeding grounds in Siberia to winter in south and south-east Asia has been found thousands of miles off course in Cumbria. Regrettably, it was found by a cat.

Chris Hind, Cumbria’s county recorder for birds, said it was “extraordinary” that a Siberian rubythroat was found on his patch, albeit dead. There have been only 17 recorded instances of the bird in the UK, 15 of those in the northern Scottish isles.

Hind was contacted by the RSPB at the weekend after a womanwho lives in a remote north Cumbrian valley reported the bird. She had recognised it was out of the ordinary and put the dead bird in her freezer.

“She realised that it was something unusual,” Hind said. “She probably didn’t realise how incredibly unusual it is. There are only two accepted records of it from mainland Britain.”

The woman, who wants to remain anonymous, told the BBC she had “heard the cat flap go” and her heart sank when her cat “brought her a bird and trotted off”.

“I saw the bird’s distinctive, beautiful orange-red feather patch under its beak but I couldn’t recognise the species,” she said.

Quite why the bird appears to have headed west, rather than south, is a mystery although Hind pointed to the puzzle of yellow-browed warblers that have been spotted in the UK in increasing numbers in recent decades.

“It used to be thought that these are birds which had just got lost but there are academics now thinking that it may be something rather more purposeful,” he said.

Hind said the bird brought in by the cat was very underweight, weighing 28 grams (just under 1oz). Textbooks say it should be about 73 grams.

Siberian rubythroats were also extremely secretive, he said. “They tend to creep around in the vegetation and the place where it was found was a place hugely vegetated, lots of trees, lots of bushes, lots of hedges in a remote little valley.”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

It was likely, he said, that no one would ever have seen it had the cat not come across it. “I have very mixed emotions. I’m very sad for the bird, quite angry with the cat, but you know cats are predators, this is what they do, it’s not the cat’s fault.”

The plan is now for academics to take samples and then the bird – currently in Hind’s freezer – will go to Tullie House museum in Carlisle. Hind hopes it can be prepared in such a way that it might go on display. As well as having a remarkable story, “it is a very dramatic looking bird”.

#167. UK ministers told not to engage formally with Indian mega-zoo after lobbying

Guardian and Mongabay reveal approaches to officials and MPs on behalf of Vantara zoo ahead of UK-chaired Cites meeting on endangered animal imports

British government ministers were told not to engage formally with Vantara, the controversial mega-zoo run by the Indian billionaire Anant Ambani, after lobbying efforts by the private animal sanctuary in the run-up to a UK-chaired meeting on the trade in endangered species, according to documents.

This week, the UK heads a 32-country committee meeting in Geneva of Cites, the United Nations body that regulates trade in endangered species. The meeting will examine how India has responded to compliance concerns about imports of endangered animals.

Documents obtained by the Guardian and the news outlet Mongabay under the UK’s Freedom of Information Act reveal Vantara’s efforts to lobby British officials and ministers before the meeting.

Ambani, 31, is the heir to the oil and gas fortune of one of India’s richest men, Mukesh Ambani. He set up a 1,400-hectare (3,500-acre) private zoo near his family’s oil-refining facilities in Gujarat, where more than 150,000 animals have reportedly been amassed since 2022. Vantara boasts high welfare standards for animals in its care, including facilities such as an elephant hydrotherapy pool, where more than 260 elephants can also receive oil therapies and Ayurvedic massage.

But conservation groups have repeatedly questioned how such a vast number of species, many of which are endangered, were gathered so quickly. Last year, India’s supreme court cleared the zoo’s operators of any wrongdoing.

A subsequent Cites fact-finding mission looking into the zoo raised concerns about India’s compliance with international rules on animal imports in 2025. It recommended temporary restrictions on India, although the recommendation was later withdrawn.

Its November 2025 report said: “There is a need for caution to ensure that imports of large numbers of animals by these facilities are not inadvertently creating a demand for illegally sourced animals.”

In May, officials from the UK’s Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra) met a representative from Vantara and the US lobbyist firm Checkmate Government Relations, founded by Ches McDowell, a hunting friend of Donald Trump Jr.

Before the meeting, internal briefing documents said Checkmate was “seeking to persuade the UK to take a more sympathetic view of India’s handling of imports to Vantara and of Checkmate’s argument that responsibility for verifying legality sits primarily with exporting or re-exporting parties rather than with India as importer”.

During the meeting, the Vantara representative invited UK officials to visit the zoo – an invitation that was subsequently declined to ensure the impartiality of the UK’s Cites chair role.

After the meeting, a representative from another lobbying firm, Atlas Strategy, working on behalf of Vantara, approached the then secretary of state, Emma Reynolds, and then nature minister, Mary Creagh, also raising the prospect of a visit to the zoo.

When a department official asked for advice, a note was issued recommending that ministers did not engage formally with Vantara.

The government documents also reveal concerns about an application by Vantara to import falcons and falcon hybrids, after an unnamed organisation made a £50,000 “donation” to a breeder in the UK, whose identity was also not disclosed. British Cites officials classified the transaction as commercial, according to the documents. The falcons were not exported to India.

A spokesperson for Vantara said it had not imported falcons from the UK, nor had the zoo hired or paid a lobbying firm. It is understood that Checkmate and Altas Strategy were working pro bono for Vantara and not required to register with the Office of the Registrar of Consultant Lobbyists.

After recent scrutiny, Vantara has sought to “reset” its activities by voluntarily suspending animal imports for a year and hiring John Scanlon, a former Cites chief, to oversee its operations. In an interview with the Sunday Times this year, Ambani said he only brought animals to Vantara legally to ensure their welfare, mentioning a group of hippos he had saved from a hunting lodge in South Africa and jackass penguins saved from a shopping centre in Qatar.

“I think since I was a baby I have been taking care of animals. It’s always been a passion,” he said.

Vantara was used to host Ambani’s lavish pre-wedding celebrations in 2024, which were attended by celebrity guests and politicians. In September, Newcastle University awarded the oil heir a professorship of practice for “his work in conservation, biodiversity protection, animal welfare and environmental stewardship”, sparking a backlash from conservationists.

A Defra spokesperson said: “The UK has strict rules in place to protect endangered species and ensuring international wildlife trade is legal and sustainable, with every application assessed individually against internationally agreed and domestic requirements.”
