#139. NAZA: how and where to watch the Guardian’s acclaimed Gaza documentary

Film features testimony from 24 Israeli officials involved in surveillance and remote killing of Palestinian civilians

NAZA will be released in full on the Guardian’s website for free in early December. Sign up here to be notified of the exact streaming date and to be sent a link to the film when it is live.

NAZA is currently screening in cinemas all over the world. It has been shown by cinemas in 47 countries and seen by more than 1.5 million people since its release last month, making it one of the most-watched documentary films with a theatrical release in 2026. The film has been offered to a number of distributors and cinemas in Israel but has not been screened there yet.

NAZA is a documentary featuring testimony from 24 Israeli military intelligence officers and soldiers involved in the surveillance and remote killing of Palestinian civilians during the war in Gaza. The film’s title comes from an Israeli military acronym used euphemistically to refer to civilians expected to be killed in a military attack.

The interviewees detail the methods used by the Israeli military in Gaza, including AI-based systems built to identify large numbers of potential Hamas operatives, who were then bombed in their homes at night, in the knowledge that the attacks would also kill their families.

Interviews were filmed at night on rooftops in Tel Aviv, on the condition of anonymity, because of the risks involved in speaking publicly. The appearance and voices of participants in the film have been digitally altered to disguise their identities.

The film was directed by the Oscar-winning Israeli directors Yuval Abraham and Rachel Szor and produced by the Guardian. It builds on revelations first published by the Israeli-Palestinian magazine +972, the Hebrew-language news outlet Local Call and the Guardian newspaper between 2023 and 2025.

Abraham and Szor were two of the four co-directors – with the Palestinians Basel Adra and Hamdan Ballal – of No Other Land, which documents the destruction of Masafer Yatta, a village in the occupied West Bank targeted by Israeli forces. It won the Oscar for best documentary in 2025.

NAZA was co-produced by James Wilson and executive-produced by Jonathan Glazer, the team behind the Oscar-winning film The Zone of Interest.

NAZA has received widespread critical acclaim and is selling out in cinemas across the world. The New York Times says it is “tough to watch and impossible to ignore”, while New York Magazine describes it as “one of the most important films of our time”. In a five-star review, the Financial Times says that “journalism and film-making brilliantly entwine”. British Vogue calls NAZA a “brilliant and harrowing film”.

The film won the special jury prize at the Venice film festival in September, where its premiere had a 25-minute ovation – a festival record. In November, it won the best political documentary from the Critics Choice Association.

The film, its directors and their confidential sources have faced fierce attacks from the Israeli government and military. The prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, has ordered the attorney general and the head of the Shin Bet security service to reveal the identities of sources interviewed and prosecute them for causing “harm to Israel’s diplomatic and security interests”.

Netanyahu also threatened to strip Abraham and Szor of their citizenship, accusing them of treason. The Israeli military’s chief of staff, Eyal Zamir, said the military would “not allow soldiers to be made targets through lies and blood libels” and would use “every tool at our disposal” against the film.

Far-right groups have vowed to hound the film-makers on their return to Israel and picketed Szor’s family home. Some of the messages included explicit calls for violence, with one protester demanding the “death penalty for traitors”.

The Israeli military claimed there were “substantial errors” in the film and that it had relied on sources who had only a partial picture of the target approval process. It said that while AI was used to help identify targets in Gaza, final decisions on strikes, and the risk of civilian casualties, were signed off by officers.

In response, the team behind the film have revealed they spoke to more than 100 sources with knowledge of the military systems used in the war.

The Israeli government is promoting an online film project, called NAZA: The Real Story, which mimics the style of the genuine NAZA film and is hosted on a website called nazadocumentary.com. It is not the real NAZA documentary and has been misleading some viewers.

Initially, the rival film’s website falsely presented itself as a promotional page for NAZA itself, naming Abraham and Szor as the directors, and listing film festivals where the documentary was to be screened. The site now appears as a platform for the rival film, which has been promoted by Israeli embassies.

Ariel Averbuch, an Israeli attorney and army reservist who co-created NAZA: The Real Story, describes it as an “offensive move” to shift the world’s focus from allegations about the Israeli military’s killing of civilians.

Szor and Abraham have addressed the backlash to their documentary. “Our country is doing so much to hide these systems,” Szor said.

NAZA is in cinemas worldwide and will screen for free on the Guardian’s website from early December. Sign up here to be the first to watch it

#133. Two men found guilty of helping Iran try to ‘eliminate’ UK-based journalists

Mostafa Sepahvand and Farhad Javadi Manesh carried out surveillance in an effort to inflict violence on targets

Two men of Iranian origin who were hired through social media as part of a plan to “eliminate” journalists in the UK have been found guilty of assisting Tehran’s intelligence services.

Mostafa Sepahvand, 41, and Farhad Javadi Manesh, 46, carried out surveillance and reconnaissance between August 2024 and March last year in an attempt to inflict violence on “subjects” in the UK.

These subjects included two journalists for the London-based Persian broadcaster Iran International, Mojtaba Pourmohsen and Kambiz Hosseini, and a founder member of UK Lawyers for Israel, Jonathan Turner.

The defendants visited addresses linked to their targets, took photos and videos and used platforms such as ChatGPT to research them online. They appear to be part of a growing new tactic used by hostile states in which “proxies” are hired to work for foreign security forces, with often little idea who they are targeting or why.

Last month the Norwegian teenager Johannes Natland, 19, was jailed for life after he agreed to carry out a hit in Huddersfield for the Foxtrot Network, a Swedish organised crime group backed by the Iranian regime.

The Iranian men came to the UK illegally between 2016 and 2022 – Sepahvand in the back of a lorry and Manesh in a small boat.

Sepahvand used the AI platform ChatGPT to find the home address of Pourmohsen, who worked for the London-based Persian language broadcaster Iran International. He hoped to be paid £150,000 to carry out a violent attack and help to “finish off” Pourmohsen.

Sepahvand chatted on WhatsApp to his handler, an Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps commander called Amin, as he spied on Pourmohsen in December 2024, Woolwich crown court heard.

Amin wrote: “These are new pictures of the guy/bloke.”

Referring to a picture, Amin said: “And that dot is their office?”

Sepahvand said: “Love you brother, I’ll go there tomorrow.”

The pair also discussed surveillance of the Iranian satirist Kambiz Hosseini, who eventually had to leave home because of the threats made against him.

Bill Emlyn Jones KC, prosecuting, said Iran and its rulers did not and could not tolerate dissent. “One organisation critical to the regime, and therefore very much in its sights, is Iran International, a news organisation which has come under particular attack because of its explicit support for opposition groups and its editorial stance, which is anti-Islamic Republic of Iran.

“Eliminating hostile, critical or dissident actors overseas is a staple policy of the Islamic republic. Together, the defendants were engaged in what is very obviously a form of spying,” Jones said.

Hosseini, 51, had to leave home and stop work as a standup comedian as a result of the threats he received. He was sent a message with a picture of his car saying the next time he started the engine it would blow up. Hosseini’s family in Iran also received threats, and his family had to leave the country.

The court heard Sepahvand had been convicted of theft in Iran and was drawn into the plan to “eliminate” dissidents in the hope of reducing his criminal record.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

He told his Iranian handlers he was prepared to escalate from surveillance to violence, the court heard.

He hoped to receive a final payment of £150,000 for “finishing the job”, a reference to killing one of the journalists, the court was told. “If the person [the target] is inside, they pay the £150,000, and I’ll finish him off,” said Sepahvand in a voice note.

Sepahvand claimed his family was being threatened and that he never intended to go through with the hit. He also denied plotting to kill Turner.

Manesh claimed he took a picture of the Iran International site because it was “a beautiful building” and did not know they were working with the Iranian intelligence services.

Earlier this year, Roman Lavrynovych, 22, and Stanislav Carpiuc, 27, were jailed at the Old Bailey for seven and two years respectively for arson attacks on property connected to Keir Starmer. An anonymous Russian-speaking figure named El Money had recruited Lavrynovych and Carpiuc on Telegram.

Last year, six Britons were jailed for their parts in a Russian-ordered arson attack on a London warehouse providing aid to Ukraine. The ringleader, Dylan Earl, was recruited by the Wagner group, a mercenary organisation that acts on behalf of the Russian state.

Sepahvand was convicted by the jury of assisting a foreign intelligence service and preparatory conduct. Manesh was convicted of assisting a foreign intelligence service, but cleared of preparatory conduct.

Shapoor Noori, 57, of Ealing, was cleared by the jury of assisting a foreign intelligence service and preparatory conduct.

Sepahvand, Manesh and Noori had claimed asylum in the UK and received temporary leave to remain. None of them had regular full-time employment.

It is understood they met by chance and were then directed by Sepahvand and his contacts in Iran. There appeared to be no evidence of any wider plot.

Mrs Justice Cheema-Grubb remanded Sepahvand and Manesh before sentencing at the Old Bailey on 17 December.

#130. Boxer on death row in Iran vows ‘not to bow before oppression’ after winning rights prize

Political prisoner Mohammad Javad Vafaei Sani, one of seven athletes at risk of execution in Iran, given prestigious Magnitsky humanitarian award

A boxer and political prisoner on death row in Iran said he will “never surrender” as he accepted a prestigious human rights prize on behalf of “the generation that is paying the price of freedom with their lives”.

Mohammad Javad Vafaei Sani, an Iranian boxing champion, coach and political prisoner, has been sentenced to death three times and is one of seven athletes at risk of execution in Iran. He was given the Magnitsky award for outstanding human rights activist at a ceremony in London on Thursday night.

In a written message from Vakilabad prison in north-eastern Iran, he said: “I accept this award in the name of all those who, for the ‘crime’ of aspiring to a free Iran, have been imprisoned, tortured, threatened, or sentenced to death; in the name of my comrades in the resistance units and the generation that is paying the price of freedom with their lives.”

He added: “The regime demands repentance, televised confessions and surrender from us. But our answer is clear: ‘We will never surrender; we will never submit to humiliation.’”

Vafaei Sani, 31, was arrested in 2020 for taking part in nationwide democracy protests in 2019 and accused of supporting an opposition group, the People’s Mojahedin Organization of Iran (MEK).

He was convicted of “corruption on Earth” and sentenced to death for a third time in September 2024 after “a grossly unfair trial”, according to Amnesty International. The supreme court upheld his death sentence on 4 October 2025. Since his arrest he has been tortured and held in solitary confinement for extended periods.

His continued imprisonment has drawn widespread international condemnation from prominent sportspeople, human rights organisations and legal experts who have called for his immediate release and the annulment of his death sentence.

The Magnitsky awards are named after the Russian lawyer, Sergei Magnitsky, who died in Russian police custody after exposing government corruption. They began in 2015 as a way to recognise journalists, politicians and activists in the field of human rights.

Sir William Browder, head of the Magnitsky Justice Foundation and the chair of the awards committee, said Vafaei Sani’s case was emblematic of the plight of many Iranians who face execution for standing against the regime. “Most important, Mohammad Javad is still alive,” he said. “That is why this award matters. Every time the world pays attention, it becomes harder to kill him quietly.”

According to the Hengaw Organisation for Human Rights, at least 710 people have been executed in Iran since the beginning of 2026, including 79 political and religious prisoners.

In an October report, the organisation documented at least 106 death sentences against January protest detainees, 35 of whom had already been executed. In Vakilabad prison, where Vafaei Sani is detained, five people have been executed linked to the protests and 15 others are facing death sentences.

Vafaei Sani’s award comes at a time when there are growing fears for imprisoned Iranian athletes, who are considered role models in the country. Zohreh Abdollahkhani, founder of Human Rights for Sport, said: “The situation is really dire … We have seven athletes at risk of execution now, including Vafaei Sani.”

Ali-Sina Mostafavi, a mountaineer, was executed on 14 October after the anti-government protests. His death followed the killing of Siavash Jamshidi Kheirabadi, a taekwondo athlete, earlier that month, and the mixed martial arts fighter Gholamreza Khani Shakarab, who was hanged in May.

Abdollahkhani said people had been celebrating Vafaei Sani’s award. “Even people who don’t support his political view are supporting him to be heard,” she said. “I find it very inspiring that different political groups are coming together to say no to execution.”

In his message, Vafaei Sani said he wanted “a free and democratic Iran … where the people vote freely, women and men are equal, there is no discrimination on the basis of ethnicity, religion or belief, and no one is imprisoned or executed for political opposition”.

He added: “I remain steadfast in my pledge, and I will not bow before oppression until the people of Iran are free.”

#129. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#125. Iran says it targeted US military radar in Bahrain in retaliatory strike

First direct attack on US military installations for months was in response to US airstrike on communications tower

Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps says it has targeted and destroyed a US early-warning radar in Bahrain in response to a US airstrike on a communications tower in Sirik overlooking the strait of Hormuz. It is Iran’s first direct attack on US military installations for months.

“This is a serious warning to the aggressor enemy to end its aerial mischief and oil smuggling in the strait of Hormuz,” the IRGC said. “In the event of repeated violations and aggression, the IRGC aerospace force is on full alert to confront the enemy without constraint.”

US Central Command denied that the radar system had been destroyed and said US forces had intercepted two Iranian drones headed for Bahrain.

Donald Trump has said in a series of media interviews over the past 24 hours that he will attack Iran only if necessary, claiming that Gulf countries, such as Saudi Arabia, have urged him to hold back from planned large-scale military strikes.

Trump said he had asked the vice-president, JD Vance, to end his interest in negotiating a new deal with Iran and instead focus on corruption and fraud in Democrat-run cities and states.

Vance had been seen by the Iranians as the most trustworthy figure close to Trump, but they had rejected his request for a personal meeting with their negotiators to discuss the terms of a peace deal.

Vance has been at the forefront of Republicans saying the war was the cause of the widespread Republican losses in the midterms. Trump said he had been disappointed in the American public because higher diesel prices were a small price to pay for preventing Iran from acquiring a nuclear weapon.

An opinion poll in the UK showed 65% of the British public would oppose the US resuming the war in Iran, and only 14% would back it. The poll was conducted by YouGov for the Council for Arab-British Understanding (Caabu).

It also found 54% oppose UK bases being used by the US to mount attacks on Iran, with 29% in favour. Support for additional UK sanctions against Iran was 45%, with 22% opposed.

The poll was conducted on Tuesday and Wednesday this week, before two Iranians were found guilty on Thursday of spying and planning attacks on journalists in the UK. The Iranian embassy has rejected the court judgment as an attempt to advance a political agenda.

Chris Doyle, the director of Caabu, said: “The British public are sceptical about any war on Iran and what it might achieve. What might make the Burnham government take note is the very high level of opposition to any further war amongst 2024 Labour voters, at 79%.”

Iran’s Assembly of Experts, a body of elected Islamic jurists responsible for choosing the supreme leader, has become the first governing institution in Iran to call for a review of the country’s defence doctrine, potentially leading to religious permission for Iran to try to acquire a nuclear weapon.

Get the most important US headlines and highlights emailed direct to you every morning

In a statement released on Thursday, the assembly called for a review of Iran’s membership of the nuclear non-proliferation treaty, describing the move as an “unavoidable necessity” and a “public and national demand”.

The intervention carries a religious as much as political significance because the 88-strong assembly comprises senior clerics, most of whom are supposed to be qualified to exercise independent Islamic legal reasoning.

Iran has had a longstanding prohibition on nuclear weapons, based on a fatwa issued by the late supreme leader Ali Khamenei, but the relevance of the fatwa in the wake of the US attacks on Iran has been questioned widely in political and security circles.

Whether Iran has the stockpiles of uranium or technology to build a nuclear weapon is contested.

The UN nuclear inspectorate has expressed alarm about a continued lack of information from Iran about its nuclear sites. It said: “Our concerns are growing over Iran’s refusal to allow access to design information or inspections of the Isfahan facility. We do not know the exact location of the Isfahan uranium enrichment facility or whether it contains nuclear materials. It is imperative that we carry out our verification activities in Iran without further delay.”

#122. Putin takes hard line in US talks as Ukraine says Starlink disrupted again

Russian president says he would target any foreign troops sent to Ukraine and refuses to soften territorial demands

Vladimir Putin warned US negotiators that Russia would target any foreign troops deployed to Ukraine, in a series of hardline remarks that came as the Ukrainian military faced renewed disruptions to Elon Musk’s Starlink satellite service.

During the talks at the Kremlin with the US envoys Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff on Thursday, the Russian president refused to soften his territorial demands, insisting on full control over four Ukrainian regions that Moscow claims to have annexed.

He also ruled out accepting the deployment of foreign peacekeeping troops in Ukraine, describing such a move as a “continuation of war”.

Putin’s comments appeared to severely dampen hopes that a peace deal could be within reach, after Volodymyr Zelenskyy said there was a “window of opportunity” to reach an agreement before the end of 2026.

The latest trip to Moscow by Witkoff and Kushner, who have made numerous visits to Russia, was partly aimed at securing European participation in future negotiations, according to two people familiar with the talks.

Speaking after the meeting, Witkoff confirmed that European representatives would join the next round of talks. “They’re our partners, and it’s important for them to be involved,” he told reporters as he boarded a plane leaving Moscow.

The US envoys will now travel to Dublin, Ireland, where they will meet Zelenskyy and debrief him on the Moscow meeting.

Andy Burnham, the British prime minister, said national security adviser Jonathan Powell would join the talks in Berlin.

Amid a flurry of diplomatic activity, including the recent talks in Abu Dhabi, the Kremlin appears to have succeeded in reframing negotiations over the war in Ukraine as part of a broader discussion about European security.

But Europe is wary of Russian attempts to focus discussions on a European security architecture, while Moscow continues to pound Ukraine with bombs and missiles.

The EU foreign policy chief, Kaja Kallas, told reporters: “We already have a European security architecture in place.” Kallas pointed to the cold war-era Helsinki accords and the Budapest memorandum, which guaranteed the territorial integrity of Ukraine. “The problem is that Russia is not keeping to those agreements, so right now the focus should be that Russia actually keeps to their obligations … and they don’t attack another country again.” Focusing on a European security architecture was a “distraction” without a ceasefire, she added.

Both Washington and Moscow have been urging European leaders to appoint a single representative to lead negotiations on their behalf.

“Once a representative is appointed, a chief negotiator authorised to speak on Europe’s behalf, there will be a four-way dialogue,” Putin said on Thursday.

EU leaders are due to discuss the issue of who speaks for Europe on Friday, but so far have declined to nominate one person to speak for the bloc. One EU official said the EU would be represented by its institutions when issues concerning the union were on the table, but played down any imminent appointment. They said: “Do I expect an agreement on a special representative? My answer is no, I don’t.”

Kallas suggested she – as the EU’s high representative for foreign and security policy – should represent the EU in talks, alongside António Costa, the president of the European Council.

More than 45 European leaders are meeting in Dublin for the European Political Community summit, a gathering that brings together nearly every country on the continent, apart from the leaders of Belarus and Russia.

The French president, Emmanuel Macron, played down the importance of having a single European negotiator, saying “the content determines the format”. “There is no more US support. So there [are] some mediations, fine,” he said, referring to the process led by Kushner and Witkoff. “But at the end of the day we will be the one to define and decide for our future security as Europeans.”

The German chancellor, Friedrich Merz, said it was first necessary to get Putin to the negotiating table before deciding on a European envoy. Merz said: “It’s not about individuals. What matters is that Putin is prepared to hold talks.”

Meanwhile, Zelenskyy accused Musk’s Starlink satellite service of damaging Ukraine’s ability to defend itself against Russian drone attacks, after Ukrainian military officials reported new restrictions on the system.

According to Zelenskyy, Starshield, a military version of Starlink funded by the Pentagon, has for several days been subject to speed limits of 100mph (161km/h) along the frontline and in Russian-occupied territory.

Similar restrictions last month prompted confusion over who had ordered them, with Musk and the Trump administration pointing fingers at each other before the service was partly restored.

The latest restrictions had made it harder for Ukrainian forces to intercept Russia’s increasingly sophisticated jet-powered drones, Zelenskyy said, accusing the service of “helping the Russians kill more Ukrainians and carry out more accurate strikes against Ukraine”.

Kyiv has contacted Washington to seek an explanation for the disruption, which Zelenskyy said appeared to be deliberate rather than a technical fault.

#120. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#118. More than 60% of Germany’s female soldiers report harassment or abuse

Defence minister says extent of sexual misconduct found by study is ‘unacceptable’, as military tries to attract more recruits

Germany’s defence minister has promised a “zero tolerance” approach to sexual abuse in the armed forces after a study found soldiers, particularly women, faced extensive harassment within the ranks.

The first comprehensive study of its kind into the Bundeswehr, conducted by the Centre for Military History and Social Sciences in Potsdam and released this week, comes as the military is trying to attract new recruits in response to global threats.

It found that more than 60% of female soldiers had reported experiencing some form of sexual harassment or abuse while on duty, ranging from sexually explicit remarks and degrading rituals to sexual violence.

Nearly 40% of the 4,000 women who responded to the survey said they had been addressed with suggestive comments and sexualised remarks, while 15% reported being harassed with mobile phone pictures taken against their will and in some cases altered using AI.

Just over 8% reported having been physically attacked, and 2% said they had been the victims of sexual violence.

Around 19% of male ‌soldiers had experienced a similar form of sexual misconduct, with non-binary or gay soldiers disproportionately affected, the study found.

Boris Pistorius, the defence minister, said the figures were “unacceptable”, in particular for a military that is trying to considerably increase its numbers. The Bundeswehr has pumped billions into boosting both personnel and equipment. Military bosses have set a target of 260,000 active-duty troops and 200,000 reservists by 2035, in the hope of avoiding the introduction of conscription.

Pistorius said he was struck by the finding that women in units with a higher proportion of other women were less frequently affected, which he said “argues in favour of recruiting more women into the Bundeswehr”.

He said he had been advised by some colleagues to ditch the study, over fears it would sully the reputation of the military. To do so, he said, would be akin to “turning a blind eye”, which in the long term would do more damage to the Bundeswehr. The study would be repeated every year, he added.

“Zero tolerance applies to sexual harassment within the Bundeswehr,” the minister said.

Pistorius expressed concern over a high level of reluctance to report incidents. About a third of those surveyed said they did not or would not report incidents because of a lack of trust in what their superiors would do.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

Before the study results were made public, Pistorius set up a hotline this month, meant to serve military personnel, which is to be staffed around the clock, and is called HELP.

In future, Pistorius said, those who made complaints should receive more information about the status of their proceedings, after victims said they were often left in the dark, but expected to continue serving.

The ministry said that, of 45,000 active or former members of the armed forces contacted to participate in the survey, 18% responded. It said its analysis showed that the figures were nevertheless representative.

#115. Is the AI bubble bursting just as Anthropic goes public?

The sharp sell-off in tech stocks after the US midterms has cast doubt on Anthropic's looming IPO. Yet, with hundreds of billions still being invested, some think the AI party is still in full swing.

2026 was meant to be the year Wall Street’s trillion-dollar artificial intelligence (AI) bet started paying off. Instead, it’s turning into a test of nerves.

In the first half of the year, many AI stocks hit record highs as US hyperscalers like Google's Alphabet, Meta and Microsoft recorded bumper earnings.

Two of the largest AI players — Anthropic and OpenAI — announced plans for public listings, following SpaceX's much-hyped IPO in June, which briefly made Elon Musk the world's first trillionaire.

AI models, meanwhile, got noticeably better at difficult tasks in the race to beat human intelligence.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The second half of 2026, however, has brought a lot more uncertainty. While the stock of Nvidia, a chipmaker, pushed even higher last month, many hyperscalers and chip or memory firms have seen their stock prices gyrate wildly.

Then last week, after the Democrats secured control of the United States Congress in the midterm elections, technology stocks sold off dramatically, with the Magnificent Seven — including Meta, Apple, Amazon and Tesla — losing more than $1 trillion in value in a single trading day.

In a further twist, the AI sector took another hit this week as Nvidia shares plunged nearly 10% over two days. The sell-off followed news that Nvidia's board — alongside the FBI — is investigating CEO Jensen Huang’s ties to a woman suspected by US intelligence of being a Chinese spy.

On Thursday, Huang resigned from the chipmaker when a second alleged marital affair was revealed, sending Nvidia's shares lower still.

Wall Street is growing increasingly cautious about the sheer size of the AI outlay, amid warnings that future earnings may not justify the huge spending. Growing pressure on US lawmakers to curb the expansion of AI data centers has added to those doubts.

In September, investment bank Goldman Sachs estimated that hyperscalers need around $300 billion in annual AI revenue to break even on their spending, leaving a gap of roughly $230 billion.

"We're not in Kansas anymore," Dennis Dick, chief market strategist at the Canada-based Stock Trader Network, told DW, meaning that times have changed. Now, he said, investors are telling companies: "show me the money."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Other analysts think it's too soon to expect the AI bubble to burst, including Kit Rees, investment strategist at London-based wealth manager Quilter, who thinks last week's rout was a "healthy correction."

"We’re still in the very early innings of the AI build-out," Rees told DW. "There’s still a lot of excitement around this technology and investors will want to have a slice of the pie."

In addition to the roughly $800 billion invested in capital expenditures this year, AI hyperscalers plan to spend a further $1.2 trillion in 2027 and $1.4 trillion in 2028, according to Goldman Sachs estimates.

While OpenAI has delayed its public listing until next year, Claude-maker Anthropic still plans its IPO later this month. On Tuesday, the company said it would offer 825 million shares at between $70 and $78 per share.

Dick said Anthropic’s decision to go ahead amid the market sell-off left him scratching his head. “Is this the best timing?” he asked.

While Anthropic had originally targeted a valuation of over $2 trillion, it now thinks the firm could be worth around $1.2 trillion to $1.3 trillion.

Dick said the valuation depends on whether Anthropic can deliver on a forecast by Goldman Sachs of over $600 billion in revenue by 2030.

"If you get to a company with $600 billion in revenue, [a] $1.3 trillion [valuation] doesn’t sound that bad," he told DW. "But when you’re talking about a company with currently $52 billion in revenue, it sounds like it’s overvalued."

Other analysts, like Vivek Seth, chief executive officer of Shaires Holdings, are more bullish about the Claude-maker's prospects.

“Anthropic is a once-in-a-generation company,” Seth told Bloomberg this week, noting how it has grown rapidly and has strong institutional backing. “Its IPO will establish a market-clearing valuation as conditions stabilize.”

With many investors already running for the hills, Rees isn’t convinced the AI bubble will burst in the way skeptics expect. She thinks they should watch for stronger signs that the AI trade is maturing.

This could be "a slowdown in AI-related revenues for companies, or signs of less demand or less appetite for financing companies’ AI plans, be it through debt or through equity offerings," Rees told DW.

There are early signs that investors have less appetite for AI-related debt. Borrowing costs for AI infrastructure firms like CoreWeave and Oracle have soared recently. Even the cost of insuring Nvidia’s debt against default has risen 76% in a month, CNBC reported last week.

"They [Oracle] have been spending, some people say, like drunken sailors," Dick told DW. "They’ve compromised their balance sheet to a point where the stock’s fallen 65% from the highs."

That has not stopped Oracle, along with Meta, Microsoft and Alphabet, from continuing to issue new AI bonds. Since the end of last month, the four AI giants have announced deals worth $67 billion.

Rees said another sign could be any new regulations that make it harder to build out US data centers.

This summer, New York and Texas temporarily paused new data center approvals. Texas Governor-elect Gina Hinojosa, a Democrat who takes office in January, wants new laws to stop data centers’ heavy power and water use from pushing up household bills.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In his upcoming book "AI Mania: Blather, Bluster, and the Bubble of All Bubbles," economist Gary Smith compares the AI boom with earlier frenzies around railroads, automobiles, television and the internet. He warns of a "frightful crash" when the AI bubble eventually pops.

When the dot-com bubble burst in 2000, the tech-heavy Nasdaq stock market index lost nearly 80% of its value. Yet, previous bubbles have often lasted far longer than expected, making the timing of any crash hard to predict.

Joachim Klement, head of market strategy at UK-based investment bank Panmure Liberum, believes a reckoning could happen as early as next year.

"My core conviction is that the AI bubble will either burst in 2027 or in 2028, so sometime in the next two years,” Klement told Bloomberg last month.

→ Baca artikel asli

#114. Children’s exposure to home wood-burning could be as significant as traffic exhaust, study suggests

Research finds highest levels of cancer-related PAH pollutants in cities that have widespread wood heating

Children’s exposure to home wood-burning could be as significant as exposure to traffic exhaust, suggests new research across six European towns and cities.

Scientists measured the air in buildings where children spend much of their time; in schools, sports centres and homes. Specifically, they looked at polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAH), a family of pollutants that are given off when fuels are burned.

Dr Barend van Drooge, from Barcelona’s Institute for Environmental Assessment and Water Research (IDAEA) and senior author of the study, said: “Some of these compounds are related to cancers after prolonged exposures, as recognised by the International Agency for Cancer Research, and evidence exists that they delay cognitive development.”

Measurements were made in and around Athens, Barcelona, Colchester, Copenhagen, Helsinki and Lisbon.

Van Drooge continued: “Urban areas with wood-burning, such as Colchester and Athens, had the highest PAHs levels. Barcelona and Lisbon, where wood-burning is not practised, had the lowest levels.”

In Colchester and Athens, PAH exposure was greater than the World Health Organization guidelines.

Maria Antonia Aretaki, of the IDAEA in Barcelona and first author of the study, said: “Understanding the pollutants in children’s everyday surroundings is an important step towards protecting their health. Children are particularly important given their vulnerability to environmental pollutants and the potential health implications from exposure during childhood.”

Sports centres had the lowest concentrations. This was followed by schools, through some, especially those in Lisbon, were affected by nearby busy roads. Ventilation and indoor sources were important, but the homes with some of the greatest PAH were in areas with wood-burning, and many had open fireplaces and stoves.

Winter PAH concentrations were greater than summer.

The researchers divided the PAH into two types, according to molecular weight. In the Colchester homes, the high-molecular-weight PAH in winter were more than 10 times those in summer. The low-molecular-weight PAH were around three times greater.

Van Drooge continued: “Wood-burning is known to be an important emitter of PAH, but it was surprising that it has become such an important contributor to the overall presence of PAHs in urban areas. However, none of the cities exceeded unacceptable limits for lifetime cancer risk.”

Within the UK, the impacts of wood-burning are unlikely to unique to Colchester. Separate analysis of energy performance certificates by researchers at University College London showed that Colchester ranked 120th out of 333 local councils for density of wood burners per square kilometre. Worthing, Norwich and Reading all had around four times more.

A wintertime study of 53 children from two primary schools in Anglesey (Ynys Môn) found that children living in homes with wood burners could be exposed to over three times more particle pollution than those in non-wood-burning homes. The Anglesey study also found that homes had more air pollution than schools.

In Greece, wood-burning increased during the financial crisis that began in 2008. The cost of heating oil soared and many people turned to wood heating. This contributed to increased deaths associated with particle pollution in Athens, especially for those aged over 70. In some Greek towns, wood-burning leads to PAH that are 100 times greater in winter compared with summer.

#109. Russia strikes hospital in central Kyiv after SpaceX system falters again

Volodymyr Zelenskyy says speed restrictions on Starshield system made it harder for Ukraine to shoot down drones

Russia has attacked a hospital in the centre of Kyiv, close to several western embassies, after Volodymyr Zelenskyy said speed restrictions on SpaceX’s Starshield system had made it harder for Ukraine to shoot down enemy drones.

Three people were injured when a drone smashed into the National Institute of Traumatology and Orthopedics at 3am, hitting the second floor of the hospital and an adjoining 19th-century building.

Meanwhile, Russia claimed it had shot down nearly 1,200 Ukrainian drones overnight, one of the largest swarms sent by Kyiv. It reported that at least four people were killed in the southern region of Volgograd.

The blast in Kyiv blew out windows in nearby buildings including in the National Academy of Arts across the road. The six-storey hospital is located in the Shevchenkivskyi district, not far from the Danish and Romanian embassies, as well as cafes and a park popular with dog walkers.

Standing in the yard as smoke billowed from the burning building, and firefighters led dazed patients to safety, one witness, Oleksandr, said the attack had narrowly missed patients. “We were asleep when there was this massive boom. We all jumped out of bed straight away.

“There was glass all over the floor. People were running about … If it had hit a little further to the left it would have been much worse. We felt the shock wave.”

He added: “Targeting the wounded – that’s really low. Russia attacks everything. Where I live, high-rise blocks are being constantly hit by bombs. They don’t care where they drop them, or where they land.”

Russia has recently stepped up its strikes on Ukraine’s civilian infrastructure, hitting power plants, passenger trains and two bridges across the Dnipro river. Ukraine says the attacks are part of a campaign of terror, designed to make a weary population capitulate.

On Thursday, Zelenskyy said the Starshield system – operated by Elon Musk’s SpaceX and overseen by the Pentagon – was again experiencing significant disruptions, after an unexplained slowdown last month.

He said some Russian aerial sorties had got through because Ukrainian interceptor systems that rely on the US satellite service could not reach the necessary speed to knock out enemy drones. Starshield is the Ukrainian military’s version of Starlink.

The White House has yet to explain why the service has been throttled for a second time, but it comes after intense US-led shuttle diplomacy this week, with Donald Trump seemingly pressuring Zelenskyy to make territorial concessions to Russia’s president, Vladimir Putin.

Trump’s emissaries, his son-in-law Jared Kushner and the real estate developer Steve Witkoff, met Putin in Moscow on Thursday. They then flew to Dublin to brief Zelenskyy on their talks, which came after trilateral negotiations on Tuesday and Wednesday in Abu Dhabi.

Kremlin negotiators insist that Ukraine hand over four Ukrainian provinces, including cities and villages Russian troops have been unable to conquer. They are also calling for a new “security architecture” in Europe – a demand in effect that Nato go back to its pre-1997 posture, before eastern European nations joined the alliance.

At the same time, Moscow does not want European states to be involved in US-brokered talks to end the war in Ukraine. On Thursday, France’s president, Emmanuel Macron, said Europe had to take part since “US support” for Kyiv had ended.

“Without Europeans, there is no support for Ukraine,” Macron said. “At the end of the day, we will be the ones to define and decide for our future security as Europeans.”

“Energy workers ​are working at an intensified pace, ⁠but new attacks ​and constant ​air raid ​alerts ​are ‌complicating and delaying ​repairs,” ​Nekrasov said.

#108. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#105. Europe should not walk into Russia’s ‘traps’ on Ukraine talks, EU’s foreign policy chief Kallas says – Europe live

Kaja Kallas warns against agreeing to Russia’s proposed sequence of events on talks on Ukraine

EU foreign policy chief Kaja Kallas has warned against the EU agreeing to Russia’s proposed sequence of talks on Ukraine, saying it would allow Moscow to “divert the attention away” from its actions and ultimately leave Europe “in a worse place.”

She welcomed the US side “finally admitting what I said already one and a half years ago – that for any deal between Russia and Ukraine … you also need the Europeans.”

“I hope that we are just not at the table, but we are actually also really influencing the outcome of these negotiations.”

“The most important is that we keep to our agreement that first the ceasefire, then peace agreement and then any kind of other talks … when Russia is still presenting their maximum demands, then we should also stick to the maximum demands and start to negotiate from there. …

Kallas warned that there is a risk of Europeans “actually walking into the traps that Russia is setting” as Moscow is trying to “divert the attention away from that there is no ceasefire and actually [get us to] start to listen to their claims.”

“If we walk into that trap that we start to discuss … the European security architecture, then I don’t think we are in a better place. We are actually in worse place.”

“Let’s focus on what it is at hand. We have one aggressor and one victim. The aggressor does not have security concerns, as nobody has ever attacked them. They have attacked others. I mean, keep the focus, people.”

Kallas is also very clear that she wants to be part of the EU negotiating team, saying she was appointed to represent Europe on foreign and security policy.

“I know that the Russians don’t want me, but do we have a say on who is representing Russia? No, we don’t.”

She also said that ultimately she would not be “representing myself,” but bound by any mandate given to her by the 27.

Slightly amused/frustrated with repeated questions on what’s holding up the EU with deciding on its representation, she eventually hits back with this line:

“Well, we have institutions in place, so we can’t just send any person that has no institutional role like the Americans are sending. Clearly, we should not copy the same patterns.”

Asked why she thinks the Russians do not want her to be involved in the negotiations, she says:

“They don’t want [anyone] strong on the other side because they want to push their through their maximum demands, like they have always done. Because … their tactics is very clear: at first you present threats and ultimatums, you use force, and then you will just wait, because there will always be people in the west who will offer you something you didn’t have before. And every time you go further.

So, of course, if you negotiate with anybody, you want the other side to be as weak as possible so that you can get what you want.”

The comment by Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov suggests Russia has not yet committed to including Europeans in the next round of talks, despite US negotiators saying on Thursday that president Vladimir Putin ⁠had agreed to their participation, Reuters said.

The party – which could decide the shape of the next government – has asked the leaders of the centre-left Social Democrats and centre-right Moderates to share their proposals on how they could work together in office.

The deadline expires this afternoon, and hopefully the exercise will get us a bit closer to resolving the current standoff.

He said the discussion on who should represent Europe is “slightly overstated,” as the focus should be on Europe’s interests, not personalities.

He said that some of the issues are “pure EU interests” to be represented by EU institutions, but others should involve the largest countries, including also the UK.

“I am quite flexible on that. I think what we actually say is more important than the exact configuration of the people in the room.”

“The most important is that we keep to our agreement that first the ceasefire, then peace agreement and then any kind of other talks … when Russia is still presenting their maximum demands, then we should also stick to the maximum demands and start to negotiate from there. …

Kallas warned that there is a risk of Europeans “actually walking into the traps that Russia is setting” as Moscow is trying to “divert the attention away from that there is no ceasefire and actually [get us to] start to listen to their claims.”

“If we walk into that trap that we start to discuss … the European security architecture, then I don’t think we are in a better place. We are actually in worse place.”

“Let’s focus on what it is at hand. We have one aggressor and one victim. The aggressor does not have security concerns, as nobody has ever attacked them. They have attacked others. I mean, keep the focus, people.”

Kallas is also very clear that she wants to be part of the EU negotiating team, saying she was appointed to represent Europe on foreign and security policy.

“I know that the Russians don’t want me, but do we have a say on who is representing Russia? No, we don’t.”

She also said that ultimately she would not be “representing myself,” but bound by any mandate given to her by the 27.

Slightly amused/frustrated with repeated questions on what’s holding up the EU with deciding on its representation, she eventually hits back with this line:

“Well, we have institutions in place, so we can’t just send any person that has no institutional role like the Americans are sending. Clearly, we should not copy the same patterns.”

Asked why she thinks the Russians do not want her to be involved in the negotiations, she says:

“They don’t want [anyone] strong on the other side because they want to push their through their maximum demands, like they have always done. Because … their tactics is very clear: at first you present threats and ultimatums, you use force, and then you will just wait, because there will always be people in the west who will offer you something you didn’t have before. And every time you go further.

So, of course, if you negotiate with anybody, you want the other side to be as weak as possible so that you can get what you want.”

It’s a pretty chilly morning in Dublin, but the European Council president, António Costa, is keen to signal to his hosts that he’s ready to do business, showing off a cute Irish green scarf to Ireland’s Micheál Martin.

Today’s host, Ireland’s Micheál Martin, said discussions will focus on what Europe needs to consider any future Ukraine settlement a just and sustainable peace.

“The substance of any peace agreement is as important as who represents Europe. Europe needs to be very clear in terms of what would constitute a sustainable peace for the continent, well into the future. Security guarantees are a significant part of that, and also the reconstruction of Ukraine.”

He said the outstanding questions on Europe’s priorities and representation need to be decided “in the next week or two.”

On AI, he said the meeting will “explore the implications of AI, positive … plus the potential negatives, and the balance between regulation and innovation.”

Estonian prime minister Kristen Michal has warned that Ukraine faces “winter … like hell again,” with Russia “bombing everything” as he urged European leaders to double down on their backing for Kyiv.

“Putin has to change his goals because the war, aggression, and killing of women, children, and civilians would stop right now if he gave an order.”

“The European plan for a while [has been] quite simple: help Ukraine push Russia and so should it be.”

Michal warned that Putin’s attempt to discuss the broader European security architecture before agreeing to end the war is “a mental trap” by Russia’s Putin.

“Does anybody really think that Europe is a threat to Russia? This is not sane talk to be honest.

So, I would say that our red line is quite clear: we should have peace, on Ukraine’s terms, and after that everything can be talked, but not before and not at the same time.”

The Estonian PM also said that if the EU needs to send its negotiators, the European Council president António Costa and EU foreign policy chief Kaja Kallas are “the persons to do it”.

European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen insisted it is “self-explanatory” that Europe should be included in the peace talks on Ukraine “to safeguard our interests, but also to support Ukraine.”

“We will come forward as a commission with a proposal for the financing of the Ukrainian needs in 2027, and with the 22nd sanctions package before the next European Council,” she said.

von der Leyen also said today’s talks would focus on “the risks … but also the opportunities’ associated with the rise of AI.

EU leaders will continue talks on their diplomatic and financial support for Ukraine today, after Volodymyr Zelenskyy urged them to “pay attention” to Vladimir Putin’s demands on security.

European leaders meeting in Dublin on Thursday rejected calls that there was any need to recreate a European security architecture, while restating that Russia needed to stop attacking Ukraine.

European Council president António Costa summed it up:

“There is no need to reinvent the wheel. Europe had a functioning European security architecture until it was first, gradually undermined, and ultimately destroyed by Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.”

Speaking to reporters the UK prime minister Andy Burnham said Europe “in the wider sense” i.e not just the EU, would decide on its own arrangements.

“In terms of the wider point, there was a very strong feeling that you know, Russian demands to put the question of European security architecture or European security arrangements into this negotiation – that is not up for negotiation... It is for Europe to decide its own arrangements. Europe and Europe alone. And again, I am talking in the wider sense when I say that.”

Around 40 countries, most of the attendees of the European Political Community summit on Thursday, signed a declaration drafted by France declaring that European states “bear a primary responsibility and are assuming greater responsibility for the security of Europe”.

EU leaders are continuing talks in Dublin on Friday, focusing on AI. But following interventions from Giorgia Meloni and Friedrich Merz, they will also discuss Europe’s role in international peace talks and the question of future financial support for Ukraine.

EU leaders are meeting in Dublin today for discussions on Ukraine and AI, among others, as they continue their search for a lead EU negotiator in any peace talks with Russia.

Their discussions are becoming increasingly urgent, after Ukraine and Russia have agreed to expand the next round of peace negotiations to include European countries, US envoy Jared Kushner said last night after meeting Ukraine’s Volodymyr Zelenskyy in Dublin.

“This will allow us to address the broader postwar European security arrangements alongside the territorial issues between Ukraine and Russia,” Kushner ​said ​later on X.

But, as he somewhat mockingly said on Wednesday,

“If Europe could identify one person to speak on their behalf, that would be a bigger achievement than getting the Russians to include them in the negotiations.”

Let’s see what comes out of these discussions, with the leaders also expected to talk about AI.

Elsewhere, I am still keeping an eye on the Dutch government crisis as Rob Jetten works hard to avoid the collapse of his minority cabinet.

It’s Friday, 13 November 2026, it’s Jakub Krupa here, and this is Europe Live.

#104. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#102. Before Scott Colom, these two men were the last black US senators from Mississippi

Why Democrat Scott Colomâs win in Mississippi Senate race is so significant

After winning a close race in Mississippi, Democrat Scott Colom will become the state's first black US senator for more than 150 years, and the third in history.

It is a historic result in the deeply conservative southern state that last sent a Democrat to the US Senate in 1982, and had not seated a black senator since 1875.

Colom follows Hiram Revels, who became the first black person ever to serve in the chamber when he was seated from 1870 to 1871, and Blanche Bruce, whose term ran from 1875 to 1881. Revels and Bruce were both members of the Republican Party.

Revels was chosen as Mississippi's first senator after the Civil War, serving for just over a year and campaigning strongly for racial equality.

A few years later, Bruce, who was born into slavery, became the first black person to serve a full term in the US Senate. He remained in Washington after leaving the chamber and held a role in the Treasury.

Both terms came before the repressive Jim Crow laws began to enforce racial segregation across Mississippi and other southern US states.

Blanche Bruce (L) and Hiram Revels (R) are the two previous black senators to have been chosen by Mississippi

A significant factor in Colom's victory was his success in mobilising young people and the state's large black population, who are expected to have turned out in high numbers.

In a victory speech, Colom referenced how Revels, who was a minister and served as a chaplain during the Civil War, gained support for his Senate campaign through his words.

"That's an example of the power of prayer, and through our history, there are so many people that lifted this state up through the power of prayer in very dark times," Colom said.

A portrait of Hiram Revels, taken in about 1870

Prior to the result being called, the BBC spoke to students at Jackson State University, a historically black college in Mississippi. Many of them had voted for the first time and backed Colom.

"I really feel like a change is what we need," 18-year-old Jamyria Dunn said.

"I've had some amazing days in my life," she continued, "but seeing Mississippi, this state in particular, turn blue, would really bring a light to my heart that people just would not understand."

Democrat Scott Colom makes history as Mississippi's first black senator since 19th Century
